## Reasoning

Let me analyze the mission carefully:

**The goal:** Minimize score.
- **Before switch time** (random 400-600 steps): score increases by 1 for every **stationary** robot per step → robots should be **moving**
- **After switch time**: score increases by 1 for every **moving** robot per step → robots should be **stationary**
- At switch time, a **GREEN wall block lights up**

**Optimal behavior:**
1. Before switch: all robots move (Exploration/Attraction/etc.)
2. After switch: all robots stop (Stop behavior)
3. The signal for the switch is the **GREEN light** appearing on a wall

So the ideal FSM is:
- **State 0:** Move (Exploration) while no green is visible
- **State 1:** Stop when green is perceived

The transition from moving to stopping should be triggered by **perceiving green color (C=7, l=2 for green? Let me check colors)**.

Wait, the color IDs: `--clr<I>` uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}. For condition C=7, `--l<I>x<K>` uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The problem says the wall lights up GREEN. Green is likely color 2.

The current champion is just `Stop` (B=1) with 1 state - score 11945. This means stopping all the time scores ~11945, which makes sense: 20 robots × ~500 steps before switch × 1 = ~10000, plus they're already stopped after switch (good). So roughly 10000-12000 is achievable with pure stop.

With pure Exploration (always moving): score would be ~20 robots × ~700 steps after switch = ~14000, worse.

The ideal would be: move before switch (~0 penalty), stop after switch (~0 penalty). If we can detect green perfectly, score ≈ 0.

Let me design FSMs:

1. **Elitism:** Copy champion exactly
2. **Best candidate:** State 0=Exploration, transitions to State 1=Stop on seeing green (C=7, l=2)
3. Variations of the above with different probabilities
4. 3-state versions with more nuance
5. GoToColor green variations (move toward green wall when visible - still moving, bad)

Actually GoAwayColor from green would make robots move away from the green wall... that's still moving after switch. We want Stop when green appears.

Key design: 2-state FSM: Move → Stop on perceiving green (l=2, high probability). Also need a way back? No - once green appears, stay stopped.

For the transition back (in case of false triggers), maybe low probability random transition from Stop to Move.

FSM: 1 state = Stop (champion, score 11945)
FSM: 2 states: Exploration → Stop on green perception

- From state 2 (others=[0,1]): D=0→state0, D=1→state1

So for 3-state FSM:
State 0=Explore: K=0→D=0(State1=Stop) on green (p=0.9)
State 1=Stop: K=0→D=0(State0=Explore) on random (p=0.001)
State 2=Explore2: K=0→D=1(State1=Stop) on green (p=0.9)

FSM: --nstates 3 --s0 0 --rwm0 50 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 2 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001 --s2 0 --rwm2 20 --cle2 0 --n2 1 --n2x0 1 --c2x0 7 --l2x0 2 --p2x0 0.9

But wait, State 2 never gets entered from State 0 in this version (State 0 only goes to State 1). State 2 is unreachable. Let me add that.

State 0: K=0→D=0(State1) on green p=0.9; K=1→D=1(State2) on random p=0.001
State 1: K=0→D=0(State0) on random p=0.001 [almost never]
State 2: K=0→D=1(State1) on green p=0.9

FSM: --nstates 3 --s0 0 --rwm0 50 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --n0x1 1 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.001 --s1 1 --cle1 2 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001 --s2 0 --rwm2 20 --cle2 0 --n2 1 --n2x0 1 --c2x0 7 --l2x0 2 --p2x0 0.9

Now let me compile the full list of 14:

1. Champion (Stop, rwm57, cyan)
2. 2-state: Explore(rwm50)→Stop on green(p=0.95), Stop→random(p=0.001)
3. 2-state: Explore(rwm30)→Stop on green(p=0.9)
4. 2-state: Explore(rwm70)→Stop on green(p=0.9)
5. 2-state: Explore(rwm50)→Stop on green(p=1.0) [deterministic on perception]
6. 2-state: Explore(rwm50,yellow) with 2 transitions (green p=0.9 + random p=0.002)→Stop
7. 2-state: Attraction(att=2.0)→Stop on green(p=0.9)
8. 2-state: Attraction(att=3.0)→Stop on green(p=0.9)
9. 3-state: Explore(50)→Stop on green; Stop→Explore2 rare; Explore2(20)→Stop on green
10. 3-state: Explore(50)→Stop on green; Stop has 2 transitions; Explore2→Stop on green
11. 2-state: Repulsion→Stop on green
12. 2-state: Explore(rwm50)→Stop on green(p=0.7) [lower probability]
13. 2-state: Explore(rwm10)→Stop on green(p=0.9) [very slow turn]
14. 2-state: Explore(rwm100)→Stop on green(p=0.9) [very fast turn]

Let me write them all:

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 0 --rwm0 57 --cle0 6

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:** The champion is a 2-state FSM:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=50), transitions to State 1 (Stop) on seeing green (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) OR random (C=5, p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop, transitions back to State 0 randomly (C=5, p=0.001)

Score improved from 11945 → 8952. This confirms the strategy: move before green appears, stop after green appears.

**Important corrections:**
- LED colours: only {0, 4, 5, 6} are valid (not 1,2,3!)
- Green (colour 2) is for the camera condition `--l<I>x<K>`, NOT for `--cle<I>`
- Every state needs `--n<I>`, every transition needs destination `--n<I>x<K>`, condition `--c<I>x<K>`, and condition params

**Strategy for Round 3:**
1. Copy champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary p values, rwm, transition counts around the champion
3. Explore: try Attraction instead of Exploration, 3-state FSMs, different transition structures

The champion has p=0.9 for green detection and p=0.002 random fallback. The random fallback causes some robots to stop even before green appears (costing score). The p=0.001 return from Stop means robots occasionally start moving again after stopping.

Key improvements to try:
- Remove the random p=0.002 fallback (only stop on green)
- Higher green detection probability (p=1.0)
- Lower return probability from Stop (p=0.0001 or remove)
- Try 3-state: Explore → Stop(on green) → Explore(if green lost)
- Try Attraction as movement before green (keeps robots together for signal sharing)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Current champion:** 2-state FSM with Attraction (att=3) → Stop on green (p=0.9) or random (p=0.002), Stop → Explore random (p=0.001). Score: 7108.36.

**Analysis:** Attraction as the movement behavior (rather than Exploration) is better because robots cluster together, improving the chance that at least one robot sees the green wall and can signal via the range-and-bearing... wait, actually there's no direct signaling in AutoMoDe. But Attraction keeps robots moving (preventing stationary penalty before switch) while potentially keeping them spread enough to detect green.

**Key insight:** The score is 7108 vs optimal ~0. There are still ~7108 penalty steps. The green detection (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) triggers Stop. The random fallback (p=0.002) causes some stops before green appears. The issue might be:
1. Some robots never see the green wall (they're in the wrong position)
2. The random p=0.002 causes premature stopping

**Strategies to try:**
1. Elitism: copy champion exactly
2. Exploitation: vary att (1.0-5.0), p0x0 (green detection), p0x1 (random fallback), p1x0 (return from stop)
3. Try higher att values (robots cluster more, stay moving more)
4. Try removing the random fallback (p=0.002) - only stop on green
5. Try 3-state: Attraction → GoToColor(green) → Stop (move toward green then stop)
6. Try Repulsion as movement (spreads robots to cover arena better, more likely to see green)
7. Try lower random return from Stop (p=0.0001)

**Critical corrections from errors:**
- LED colours: only {0, 4, 5, 6}
- Every state needs `--n<I>`, every transition needs `--n<I>x<K> D` (destination), `--c<I>x<K> C`, and condition params

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion hasn't improved from round 3 to round 4, staying at 7108.36. Let me think carefully about what's happening and what could improve it.

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: Attraction (att=3), transitions to Stop on green (p=0.9) OR random stop (p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop, transitions back to Attraction randomly (p=0.001)

**Why score is ~7108:** With 20 robots and switch time ~500 steps, if robots don't see green immediately, they continue moving after the switch. Score ≈ 20 × (time after switch before detection). Also, the random p=0.002 causes ~20 × 0.002 × 500 = 20 stops per robot before switch on average... wait that's 0.002 per step × 500 steps = 1 expected premature stop per robot, each lasting ~1000 steps on average (p=0.001 return). That's costly!

**Key problem:** The random fallback p=0.002 causes robots to stop prematurely before the switch. And p=0.001 return from Stop means they stay stopped for ~1000 steps. This is very costly for the pre-switch phase.

**Better approach:** Eliminate or reduce the random fallback. Only stop on green detection. The issue is robots that never see green - but since the arena is bounded, with Attraction they cluster and some will see the green wall eventually.

**Also:** The 3-state FSM in round 3 that errored - let me try it properly. 

**Format fix:** I keep getting errors about missing `--n<I>` and `--n<I>x<K> D`. Let me be very careful. The 3-state errors are likely because when I have 3 states, I need destinations as indices into the OTHER states list, and I must include ALL required fields.

Let me carefully construct each machine with complete syntax.

**For 3-state FSMs**, destination mapping:
- From state 0: others=[1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: others=[0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2  
- From state 2: others=[0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1

**My 14 machines:**

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion with no random fallback (only green detection)
3. Champion with lower random fallback (p=0.001 instead of 0.002)
4. Champion with higher green detection (p=1.0)
5. Champion with att=5 (stronger attraction = more movement)
6. Champion with att=1 (weaker attraction)
7. Repulsion + Stop on green (spreads robots, better coverage to see green)
8. Exploration + Stop on green (no random fallback)
9. 3-state: Attraction → Stop on green; Stop → Attraction (rare); Attraction2 → Stop on green
10. 3-state: Attraction → Stop → Attraction, with green detection
11. Champion with much lower return prob (p=0.0001)
12. 2-state with neighbour condition (stop when few neighbours = spread out)
13. 2-state Attraction with higher random fallback removed, neighbour condition added
14. Champion with p0x1=0.0005 (very low random fallback)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** Score 6896.20
- State 0: Attraction (att=3), → Stop on green (p=0.9) OR random stop (p=0.001)
- State 1: Stop, → Attraction randomly (p=0.0001, very rare)

Progress: 7108 → 6896. The improvement came from reducing random fallback (p=0.002→0.001) and drastically reducing return from Stop (p=0.001→0.0001).

**What's causing the remaining ~6896 penalty?**
- Pre-switch: robots occasionally stop randomly (p=0.001 per step × 500 steps × 20 robots = 10 expected stops, each lasting ~10000 steps on average with p=0.0001 return). Wait - that's VERY costly! 10 premature stops × 10000 steps stuck = 100,000 step-robot penalties... but score is only 6896. So actually the random stops rarely happen or the run ends before they accumulate.
- Post-switch: robots that never see green keep moving. With Attraction clustering robots together, some may be far from the green wall.

**Key improvements:**
1. Remove random fallback entirely (p0x1=0 effectively - but must have valid transition). Or use p=0.0001 for random fallback.
2. Try no random fallback at all (1 transition: only green detection)
3. Try Repulsion to spread robots (better chance to see green wall)
4. Try GoToColor green → Stop (robots move toward green when seen, then stop)
5. The return from Stop at p=0.0001 is good - keep it
6. Try even lower random fallback: p=0.0001

**Important note:** I keep getting the same format errors. Let me be extremely careful:
- `--cle<I>` must be one of {0,4,5,6} - NOT 2 (green)
- Every state needs `--n<I> T`
- Every transition `--n<I>x<K>` needs a destination D right after it
- Every transition needs `--c<I>x<K> C` and condition params

For 3-state FSMs, I'll be very explicit about destinations.

**My 13 machines:**

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion with no random fallback (1 transition only: green detection)
3. Champion with p=0.0001 random fallback (even lower)
4. Champion with att=5 (stronger attraction)
5. Champion with att=2 
6. Champion with green detection p=1.0
7. Repulsion → Stop on green (spreads robots to detect green wall)
8. 3-state: Repulsion→Stop on green; Stop→Attraction(rare); Attraction→Stop on green
9. 3-state: Attraction→Stop→Attraction on green loss (if green disappears, resume)
10. 2-state: GoToColor(green)→Stop (but GoToColor keeps moving... bad). No.
10. 2-state: Attraction with neighbour condition to stop (when isolated = near wall?)
11. 2-state: Exploration→Stop on green, no fallback
12. Champion with p1x0=0 (never leave stop) - use p=0.00001
13. 2-state: Attraction att=3, green p=0.9, no fallback, return p=0.0001

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion:** Score 6295.80
- State 0: Attraction (att=5, maximum), → Stop on green (p=0.9) OR random stop (p=0.001)
- State 1: Stop, → Attraction randomly (p=0.0001)

Progress: 6896 → 6295. Higher attraction (att=5) improved things. Robots are strongly attracted to each other, keeping them moving (no stationary penalty before switch) while clustered. When green appears, they detect it and stop.

**Why att=5 is better:** Strong attraction keeps robots moving robustly (they chase each other), reducing accidental stopping. Also clusters them so they're more likely to share proximity to the green wall.

**Remaining score analysis:** ~6295 penalty steps. This likely comes from:
1. Random fallback stops (p=0.001 × ~500 steps × 20 robots = 10 premature stops, each lasting ~10000 steps) - but score is only 6295 so this isn't catastrophic
2. Post-switch robots that don't see green continuing to move
3. The p=0.001 random stop might still be hurting

**What to try:**
1. Eliminate random fallback entirely (only green detection)
2. Keep att=5 but vary green detection probability
3. Try att=5 with even lower random fallback (p=0.0001)
4. 3-state with Repulsion first (spread robots to see green wall) then Attraction
5. Try GoAwayColor(green) as initial state? No - that moves away from green.
6. The Stop state returns at p=0.0001 - could try p=0 effectively (very tiny)

**Key insight I haven't tried:** What if the initial behavior is Repulsion to spread robots around the arena, maximizing coverage, so more robots can see the green wall when it appears?

Let me design 13 machines carefully, being very precise about format.

**Format checklist for each machine:**
- `--nstates N`
- For each state i: `--s<i> B [behavior params] --cle<i> L` (L ∈ {0,4,5,6})
- `--n<i> T` (number of transitions)
- For each transition k: `--n<i>x<k> D --c<i>x<k> C [condition params]`

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion:** Score 6057.31
- State 0: Attraction (att=5), → Stop on green (p=0.9) OR random stop (p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop, → Attraction randomly (p=0.0001)

Interesting - p=0.002 random fallback is back and better than p=0.001. Progress: 6295 → 6057.

**What's still causing ~6057 penalty?**
The score of ~6057 with 20 robots over 1200 steps suggests robots are still moving for significant time after the switch. The green wall appears at switch time, but robots need to perceive it. With strong attraction (att=5), robots cluster tightly - this might actually HURT green detection since they're all bunched in the center, far from the walls.

**Key tension:** 
- Strong attraction keeps robots moving (good before switch)
- But clustering in center means fewer robots near the green wall when it lights up (bad after switch)
- Some robots may never see green and keep moving

**New ideas to try:**
1. Try a mix: start with Repulsion (spread to walls), then switch to Attraction or Stop on green
2. Try 3-state: Repulsion → Stop on green; Attraction → Stop on green (hybrid)
3. Try random fallback p=0.003 or p=0.004 (slightly higher than champion's p=0.002)
4. The p=0.002 random stop creates ~0.002×500×20 = 20 expected premature stops, each lasting ~10000 steps = problematic. But score improved so maybe the spreading effect helps detection.
5. Try GoToColor(green) as first state - when green appears robots move toward it. But they'd still be moving...

Wait - actually GoToColor(green) when green ISN'T visible probably just makes robots wander. When green IS visible, they move toward it. That's still moving = penalty after switch. Bad.

**Better idea:** What if robots start in Repulsion (spreading to walls), and switch to Stop on green? Spread robots cover more of the arena perimeter, more likely to see green wall.

**3-state idea:** 
- State 0: Repulsion (spreads robots to walls)
- State 1: Stop (when green detected)
- State 2: Attraction (alternative movement)
State 0 → State 1 on green; State 1 → State 0 rarely; State 2 → State 1 on green

**Vary p values around champion:** p=0.002→0.003, p=0.002→0.0015, att=5 is max so can't go higher.

**Important:** I must include ALL required format fields. Let me be extremely careful:

For every state i:
- `--s<i> B [params] --cle<i> L` where L ∈ {0,4,5,6}
- `--n<i> T`
- For each transition k from 0 to T-1: `--n<i>x<k> D --c<i>x<k> C [params]`

For 3-state FSMs, destination mapping:
- From state 0 (others=[1,2]): D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1 (others=[0,2]): D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- From state 2 (others=[0,1]): D=0→state0, D=1→state1

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion:** Score 5646.40
- State 0: Attraction (att=5), → Stop on green (p=0.9) OR random stop (p=0.0015)
- State 1: Stop, → Attraction randomly (p=0.0001)

Progress: 6057 → 5646. p=0.0015 is the sweet spot between 0.001 and 0.002.

**Analysis of remaining score:** With 20 robots over ~1200 steps:
- Pre-switch (~500 steps): random stops at p=0.0015 per step → expected 0.0015×500×20 = 15 robot-stops, each lasting ~10000 steps on average (p=0.0001 return). But that would be 150,000 penalty steps... clearly these don't all accumulate fully within the run. Actually each stop lasts geometrically distributed with mean 1/0.0001 = 10,000 steps, but run only lasts 1200 steps total, so a premature stop typically lasts until end of run = ~700 steps remaining. 15 stops × 700 steps = 10,500 penalty steps. But score is 5646, so maybe fewer stops happen.
- Post-switch (~700 steps): robots that don't stop keep moving. If some robots never see green, they contribute 700 steps × number of robots.

**What to try for Round 9:**
1. Exact champion copy (elitism)
2. Fine-tune p0x1 around 0.0015: try 0.0012, 0.0018, 0.0013, 0.0017
3. Try higher green detection p: 0.95, 1.0
4. Try even lower return from stop: p=0.00005, p=0.00001
5. Try 3-state with Repulsion → Stop on green (better wall coverage)
6. Try different random fallback values with p1x0 variations
7. Try att=5 with neighbour condition added to stop when few neighbours (isolated robot = near wall)

**Note on format errors:** I keep getting the same errors listed. These must be from my 3-state FSMs where I miss fields. Let me be absolutely meticulous with every 3-state machine.

For 3-state destination mapping:
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

Every state needs: `--s<i> B [params] --cle<i> L --n<i> T` then T transitions each with `--n<i>x<k> D --c<i>x<k> C [params]`.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion stuck at 5646.40 for two rounds.** The structure is solid: Attraction(att=5) → Stop on green (p=0.9) + random stop (p=0.0015), Stop → Attraction (p=0.0001).

**Final round - need to push harder.** Let me think about what's fundamentally limiting performance:

1. **Green detection coverage:** With att=5, robots cluster tightly together. When green wall lights up, only robots near that wall see it. The cluster may be far from the green wall, meaning many robots never detect green and keep moving. Solution: spread robots more before switch.

2. **The random fallback p=0.0015:** Causes premature stops. Each premature stop lasts ~10000 steps expected but run is only 1200 steps, so a premature stop at step T costs (1200-T) penalty steps. Expected cost = 0.0015 × 20 robots × integral(0 to 500 of (1200-t)/1200 dt) ≈ 0.0015 × 20 × 458 ≈ 13.7 robot-steps... wait that's tiny. Actually the run cost is much less than I thought.

3. **Post-switch robots not seeing green:** With clustered robots in center, when green appears on a random wall, only robots facing that wall detect it. The cluster might have p≈0.5 of seeing any given wall, and only robots in certain positions detect it.

**Key insight for final round:** Try Repulsion (att=5 max repulsion spreads robots to walls, maximizing chance of seeing green) combined with green detection. Or try a combination where robots start spreading then stop.

**Also try:** The 3-state FSM where state 0=Repulsion (spread), on green detected → Stop; else after some time → Attraction. But timing is tricky without explicit timers.

**Format note:** I keep seeing the same errors listed. These errors come from earlier rounds but NOT from my last submission (round 8 and 9 looked clean to me). Let me verify my 3-state format one more time.

For 3 states, from state 1 (others=[0,2]): D=0→state0, D=1→state2. From state 2 (others=[0,1]): D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

**My 12 machines for the final round:**

1. Champion (exact copy) - elitism
2. p0x1=0.0015, p1x0=5e-05 (even rarer return from stop)
3. p0x1=0.001, p1x0=1e-04 (lower random fallback)
4. p0x1=0.0015, green p=1.0 (always stop on green)
5. p0x1=0.0015, green p=0.95
6. Repulsion(rep=5) + Stop on green, p=0.0015 random
7. 3-state: Repulsion→Stop on green; Stop→Attraction(rare); Attraction→Stop on green
8. 3-state: Attraction→Stop on green; Stop→Repulsion(rare); Repulsion→Stop on green
9. p0x1=0.002, p1x0=5e-05
10. p0x1=0.0015, p1x0=1e-05
11. 2-state with neighbour-below condition: stop when isolated (near wall = far from cluster)
12. Repulsion(rep=5) + Stop on green only (no random fallback)

# PROMPT SYSTEME

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
